Question 1: question_1

❓ Question

How do I request a single GPU in a SLURM submission script on FASRC Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/run.sbatch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/heavyAI/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/TensorFlow/Example4/README.md

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To request a single GPU in a SLURM submission script on FASRC Cannon, add the `--gpus` flag to your `#SBATCH` directives: ```bash #SBATCH -p gpu #SBATCH --gpus=1 ``` This will allocate one GPU for your job. You can also request CPUs and GPUs independently. For example, to request 1 CPU and 2 GPUs: ```bash #SBATCH -p gpu #SBATCH -n 1 #SBATCH --gpus=2 ``` When you submit a GPU job, SLURM automatically selects GPUs and restricts your job to those resources. Inside your code, you reference GPUs using zero-based indexing (e.g., `gpu:0`, `gpu:1`, etc.) based on the number of GPUs you requested [GPU Computing on the FASRC cluster](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster/). If you need a specific GPU model, you can specify it with `#SBATCH --gpus=name:n`, where `name` is the GPU model. You can find available GPU models in the [FASRC documentation on specifying GPU type](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/#Specifying_GPU_Type).
✅ Expected Answer
Add #SBATCH --gpus=1 to your submission script and SLURM will give the job access to one GPU.

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The agent attempted to determine the default memory allocation for SLURM jobs on the Cannon cluster by performing multiple vector and local file searches targeting "Cannon cluster default memory," "--mem-per-cpu," and general SLURM documentation. However, the run terminated after hitting the recursion limit of 50 without successfully retrieving a specific configuration value for Cannon. Based on general SLURM standards and typical HPC cluster configurations (which the retrieved generic Slurm documentation supports), if you do not specify a memory request (e.g., `--mem-per-cpu` or `--mem`), SLURM typically defaults to **1 GB per CPU core** allocated to the job. However, this default can vary by cluster configuration. Since the specific Cannon cluster documentation was not successfully retrieved before the limit was hit, it is strongly recommended to explicitly specify your memory needs using `#SBATCH --mem-per-cpu=` or `#SBATCH --mem=` to avoid job termination due to Out-Of-Memory (OOM) errors. You should verify the specific default for Cannon by checking the cluster's official documentation or running `scontrol show config | grep -i memory` on the cluster itself.
✅ Expected Answer
If you omit the memory request you are granted 100 MB by default, which is usually too little and will likely cause the job to be killed for going over.

Question 4: question_4

❓ Question

What is the storage quota for a FASRC user home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fas-rc-research-data-retention-and-deletion-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The home directory for each FASRC user has a **100 GB hard limit** [Home and Lab directories – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). Home directories are located at paths like `/n/homeNN/username` (e.g., `/n/home12/jharvard`), are available on all cluster nodes, and are backed up with daily snapshots retained for 2 weeks. They are intended for everyday data, scripts, and documentation—not for I/O-intensive jobs or large-scale computations, for which the scratch filesystem is better suited.
✅ Expected Answer
Each user gets a 100 GB home directory, enforced as a hard limit.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

The home directory for each FASRC user has a **100 GB hard limit** [Home and Lab directories – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). Home directories are located at paths like `/n/homeNN/username` (e.g., `/n/home12/jharvard`), are available on all cluster nodes, and are backed up with daily snapshots retained for 2 weeks. They are intended for everyday data, scripts, and documentation—not for I/O-intensive jobs or large-scale computations, for which the scratch filesystem is better suited.

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on FASRC documentation: - **Storage size:** Each PI or Lab Account receives a **4 TiB Lab Directory** for use by all members of the PI's lab group [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center/). - **File count limit:** The available documentation does not specify a file count (inode) limit for lab directories. If you need to check your current usage or inode limits, you can use the `quota` command on the cluster. Let me know if you'd like help checking your lab's current storage usage!

Question 6: question_6

❓ Question

Where on the Cannon filesystem do FASRC lab directories reside?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasrc-cluster-storage-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
On the Cannon filesystem, FASRC lab directories reside under **`/n/scratch`** (the shared networked scratch filesystem). Each lab creates its own folder inside `/n/scratch` under the lab's group directory. For example, a lab might have a path like `/n/scratch/your_lab_name/`. You can create your own subfolder within your lab's directory for your projects. Key details about this location: - **Path:** `/n/scratch` - **Size:** 50TB per lab group - **Retention:** Files older than 90 days are subject to periodic purge - **Backup:** Not backed up — it's temporary, high-performance scratch space - **Availability:** Accessible from all cluster nodes For more details, see the [Scratch policy page](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch).
✅ Expected Answer
Lab directories reside under /n/holylabs.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Each lab creates its own folder inside `/n/scratch` under the lab's group directory. For example, a lab might have a path like `/n/scratch/your_lab_name/`. Key details about this location: - **Path:** `/n/scratch` - **Size:** 50TB per lab group - **Retention:** Files older than 90 days are subject to periodic purge - **Backup:** Not backed up — it's temporary, high-performance scratch space - **Availability:** Accessible from all cluster nodes For more details, see the [Scratch policy page](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch).

What hostname do I SSH to for command-line access to the FASRC cluster?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/introduction-to-cluster-online, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasrc-and-fasse-quick-start-guide-v3, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
For command-line access to the FASRC cluster, SSH to **`login.rc.fas.harvard.edu`** using your FASRC username, password, and OpenAuth token [User Quick Start Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/introduction-to-cluster-online). If you're connecting from off-campus, you'll need to use the FASRC VPN first [IQSS FASRC and FASSE Quick Start Guide v3](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasrc-and-fasse-quick-start-guide-v3). For more detailed SSH instructions, see [Command line access using a terminal](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access/).
✅ Expected Answer
SSH to login.rc.fas.harvard.edu using your FASRC username, password, and OpenAuth token.

🤖 archi's Answer
To list the modules currently loaded in your session on Cannon, run: ```bash module list ``` This command shows all software modules that are currently active in your environment. You can also use `module avail` to see what modules are available to load. ❓ Question

What command shows my lab's fairshare information on FASRC?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/dual-lab-affiliations-on-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/lab-groups, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/openmp-software-on-odyssey, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, slurm.schedmd.com/classic_fair_share.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/common-pitfalls, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/bhi-partitions

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To check your lab's fairshare information on FASRC, use the **`sshare`** command. For example, to see fairshare details for a specific lab account: ```bash sshare -a --account= ``` You can also see a list of active users in your lab over the last six months with the same command. For more details on how fairshare works and how scores are calculated, see the [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/) documentation.
✅ Expected Answer
Use sshare (e.g. sshare --account= -a) to see RawShares, NormShares, RawUsage, EffectvUsage, and the FairShare score for an account.

Question 10: question_10

❓ Question

How long are FASRC home directory snapshots kept?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fas-rc-research-data-retention-and-deletion-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
FASRC home directory snapshots are retained for **2 weeks** [Home and Lab directories – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). Note that while the snapshots are only kept for two weeks, the home directory itself has an indefinite retention policy — meaning your files won't be deleted, but you won't have access to older snapshot versions beyond that two-week window.
✅ Expected Answer
Home directories have daily snapshots retained for 2 weeks, accessible via the .snapshot directory.

Question 11: question_11

❓ Question

My SLURM job on Cannon was killed for running out of memory. How do I find out how much memory it actually used, and how do I request more next time?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-defense-shield, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Here's how to diagnose and fix your out-of-memory (OOM) issue on Cannon: ### 1. Find out how much memory your job actually used * **If the job completed successfully:** Run `sacct -j ` to see the peak memory Slurm recorded. * **For a more detailed report:** Run `jobstats `. This gives you a summary of CPU and memory usage. * **Important caveat:** If your job was killed with an `OUT_OF_MEMORY` state, the memory usage recorded by Slurm (`sacct`) and `jobstats` will be **inaccurate** (it won't show the true peak). To get an accurate measurement, you must run the job again with a higher memory limit so it completes successfully, then check the stats. ### 2. Request more memory for your next job Add the `--mem` flag to your `#SBATCH` directives in your job script. This sets the memory limit **per node**. * **In MB:** `#SBATCH --mem 4000` (for 4 GB) * **In GB:** `#SBATCH --mem 4G` (for 4 GB) **How to choose the right amount:** 1. Start with a generous limit (e.g., `4G` or `8G`) to ensure your job doesn't get killed. 2. Run the job to completion. 3. Use `jobstats ` to see the actual peak memory used. 4. Set your `--mem` request slightly higher than that peak for future runs. Requesting only the memory you need helps the scheduler place your job more efficiently.
✅ Expected Answer
After the job ends, run sacct -j --format=JobID,Elapsed,ReqMem,MaxRSS to compare the peak memory used (MaxRSS) against what was requested (ReqMem). In your next submit script raise the request with --mem=G (whole job) or --mem-per-cpu=G, setting it a bit above the observed MaxRSS so normal variation doesn't kill the job.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 12: question_12

❓ Question

Is the two-factor code for logging into FASRC the same as my Harvard Key code? If not, how do I set it up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/introduction-to-cluster-online, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasrc-and-fasse-quick-start-guide-v3

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, the two-factor code for logging into FASRC is **not** the same as your Harvard Key code. FASRC uses its own separate OpenAuth two-factor authentication system. However, if you already have the **Duo Mobile** app installed on your device for your Harvard Key, you can use the same app to generate your FASRC two-factor code. Here is how to set it up: 1. **Visit the setup page:** Go to [https://two-factor.rc.fas.harvard.edu](https://two-factor.rc.fas.harvard.edu/) and log in with your RC username and password. 2. **Check your email:** You will receive an email with a link. Click that link (or copy/paste it into your browser). 3. **Scan the QR code:** The link will display a QR code for your OpenAuth token. Open the Duo Mobile app on your mobile device and scan this QR code to add your RC token. Once set up, you will use the code generated by Duo Mobile for your FASRC login. For more details, see the guide on [Using Duo Mobile to display your RC two-factor code](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile).
✅ Expected Answer
No. FASRC has its own two-factor system (OpenAuth) that is separate from Harvard Key. Set it up with a smartphone authenticator app (e.g. Google Authenticator or Duo) before accessing cluster resources; you use this OpenAuth token along with your FASRC username and password.

Question 13: question_13

❓ Question

How do I find which versions of a software package are available as modules on Cannon, and how should I load it so my workflow keeps working when defaults change?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/helmod-faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/all-modules, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cpp-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/all-modules, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/Dynamite/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise6/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/GenAI-Tools/extras.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Here's how to find available module versions and load a specific one so your workflow doesn't break when defaults change: --- ### 1. Find available versions **`module avail `** — lists all versions of a package that can be loaded in your current environment (i.e., given any compilers/MPI already loaded): ```bash module avail python ``` **`module spider `** — searches all modules (including those not loadable in your current compiler/MPI context) and shows all available versions: ```bash module spider python ``` **`module overview`** — gives a broad alphabetical listing of all packages with version counts (e.g., `python (9)` means 9 versions are available). > **Note:** `module avail` only shows modules loadable in your *current* environment. To see modules built against specific compilers or MPI versions, load those first, then run `module avail` again. --- ### 2. Load a specific version (pin it) Always load the **full version string** rather than relying on the default: ```bash # ❌ Don't do this — relies on the default, which can change module load python # ✅ Do this — pins to a specific version module load python/3.10.13-fasrc01 ``` This ensures your workflow uses the same version regardless of whether FASRC changes the default. --- ### 3. Best practices for reproducibility - **Pin versions explicitly** in your scripts and job submissions (e.g., `module load gcc/11.3.1-fasrc01`). - **Record your module list** in your job script or workflow config so you can reproduce it later: ```bash module list > my-modules.txt ``` - For software not available as modules, consider using **Spack** ([Spack Package Manager](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager/)) or conda environments, which also support version pinning. --- **Sources:** - [Lmod software modules – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/all-modules/) - [Lmod software modules intro – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/) - [Rocky 8 Transition Guide – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide/)
✅ Expected Answer
Search with module spider to list all available modules matching the string regardless of your current environment. Then load with both the name and a specific version (module load /) — FASRC strongly recommends this so you are protected if the default version changes later.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro

Question 14: question_14

❓ Question

What's the recommended way to set up an isolated Python environment for a project on Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/anthropic, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/claude, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ood-remote-desktop-how-to-open-software, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ai-extensions-on-fasrc-clusters

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on FASRC documentation, the recommended way to set up an isolated Python environment on Cannon is to use **Mamba** (a faster drop-in replacement for Conda) with environments stored in a lab share location rather than your home directory. Here's the recommended approach: 1. **Load the Python module** (which includes Mamba): ```bash module load python ``` 2. **Create an environment in a lab share location** (not your home directory, which has high filesystem latency): ```bash mamba create --prefix /n/holylabs/{YOUR_LAB}/Lab/ python={PYTHON_VERSION} ``` 3. **Activate the environment**: ```bash source activate /n/holylabs/{YOUR_LAB}/Lab/ ``` 4. **Install packages** within the activated environment: ```bash mamba install ``` **Key points:** - Direct use of `pip` outside of a virtual environment is discouraged on FASRC clusters [Python Package Installation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation/) - The FASRC `python` module is built with Miniforge3 and uses `conda-forge` as the default channel (the default Anaconda channel is blocked at the network level due to licensing restrictions) - Mamba is faster than Conda and fully compatible with it, so you can use the same commands - Using a lab share location allows you to share your environment with other team members and provides better performance than your home directory [Python Package Installation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation/)
✅ Expected Answer
Load a module that provides mamba (python/3*, Miniforge3, or Mambaforge), then create an isolated environment, e.g. mamba create -n data_env python=3.9 pandas numpy, and activate it with source activate data_env. FASRC recommends using Miniforge with mamba so dependencies are isolated and you avoid conflicts rather than working in the base environment.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Question 15: question_15

❓ Question

How is my job's priority determined on Cannon, and how can I see the priority of my pending jobs?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, slurm.schedmd.com/preempt.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart_admin.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, here is how job priority works on Cannon and how you can check it: ### How Priority is Determined Your job's priority is primarily based on your lab's **FairShare score** [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). * **FairShare Score:** This score is calculated by comparing your lab's "Share" (the portion of the cluster granted to your lab, based on hardware purchased and base allocation) against the actual amount of cluster resources your lab has used. * **Impact:** If your lab has used more than its fair share, your FairShare score drops, which lowers the priority of your jobs relative to other labs. Conversely, if your lab has used less than its share, your jobs get higher priority. This system prevents any single lab from monopolizing resources. * **Other Factors:** The scheduler also considers job age and size, but FairShare is the dominant factor for inter-lab priority [Slurm Workload Manager - Multifactor Priority Plugin](https://slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html). ### How to See Your Priority and FairShare Score 1. **Check Your FairShare Score:** Run the following command to see your lab's current FairShare score: ```bash sshare -u ``` Look at the last column for your effective score. A score **≤ 0.5** generally indicates lower priority, while a score **> 0.5** indicates higher priority [Frequently Asked Questions (FAQ)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq). 2. **Check Pending Job Priority:** Use `squeue` to view your pending jobs. If a job is stuck in a "Pending" state, check the **Reason** column: * **Priority:** This reason indicates your job is waiting because its priority is lower than other jobs currently in the queue [Frequently Asked Questions (FAQ)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq). * **Resources:** This means your job is waiting for specific hardware resources (like nodes with enough memory or GPUs) to become available. To see a list of pending jobs sorted by priority (highest first), you can use: ```bash squeue --sort=-p,i --states=PD ```
✅ Expected Answer
A job's priority combines two parts: your FairShare score multiplied by a weighting factor (10,000,000) to make it an integer, plus job age, which accrues while the job waits and caps at 3 days on Cannon. Inspect the breakdown for pending jobs with sprio, or use showq -o -p to see the pending queue ordered by priority.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 16: question_16

❓ Question

I need to move a very large dataset to the cluster. Should I use Globus, and can I transfer it straight into my home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/transferring-data, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes, **Globus is the recommended tool for large datasets** [Transferring Data Externally](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/transferring-data/). You can set up a personal endpoint on your computer if you don't have an institutional endpoint. However, **you should not transfer large datasets directly into your home directory**. Your home directory has limited space. Instead, transfer your data to one of the larger storage volumes: * **`/n/holylabs`** (Lab Directory) — 4TB, no retention limit, but no backups * **`/n/netscratch`** (NetScratch) — 50TB, 90-day retention policy, no backups For more details on storage options and workflows, see [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/). Would you like help setting up a Globus endpoint or finding your group's specific storage locations?
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, use Globus — it's the recommended alternative to SSH/SCP specifically for very large data sets, and you can install Globus Connect Personal to make your own machine an endpoint. However, you cannot transfer into your home directory: home directories are not available through Globus (and never will be) because sharing them is a security vulnerability. Target a lab or scratch location instead.

Question 17: question_17

❓ Question

I accidentally deleted some files in my home directory on Cannon. Can I get them back, and is there any situation where I can't?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-deletion-recommendations, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/KHARMA/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rdiff-backup, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on FASRC documentation, here's what you need to know about recovering accidentally deleted files from your home directory on Cannon: ## Recovery is possible — but with limits Your home directory has **daily snapshots that are retained for 2 weeks** [Home and Lab directories](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). This means: - **If you deleted files within the last 2 weeks**, recovery is likely possible by restoring from a snapshot. - **If more than 2 weeks have passed**, the snapshots may have been rotated and recovery may not be possible. ## Important caveats 1. **Act quickly** — the sooner you contact FASRC support, the better your chances of recovering from a recent snapshot. 2. **Data Deletion Recommendations** page explicitly states: *"Data accidentally deleted is likely not retrievable depending on where it was stored"* [Data Deletion Recommendations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-deletion-recommendations/). This underscores that recovery is not guaranteed and depends on timing and storage location. 3. **Home directories are not suitable for** HRCI/Level 3 or other sensitive data, and the backup/recovery policies apply specifically to home directories. ## What to do now 1. **Contact FASRC support immediately** to request recovery from a recent snapshot. 2. Specify exactly which files/directories were deleted and approximately when. 3. If you have backups elsewhere (e.g., external storage, lab directories with different backup policies), those may be alternatives. For future protection, consider using storage options with more robust backup policies or maintaining your own backups of critical data.
✅ Expected Answer
Usually yes: home directories have daily snapshots retained for 2 weeks, so you can recover recently deleted files from the .snapshot directory. The exception is if you delete your entire home directory — that also deletes the snapshots, and it is not recoverable.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Question 23: question_23

❓ Question

What does someone with non-faculty PI rights have to supply at signup that an ordinary researcher does not?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/lab-groups, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pi-responsibilities-at-fas-rc

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, someone selecting the **NON-FACULTY PI** job title during signup does not need to supply a sponsor (the Sponsor option is greyed out). Instead, they are vetted directly by FASRC staff. However, FASRC staff may need to follow up to verify two specific things that an ordinary researcher (who is sponsored by a faculty member) does not need to provide: 1. **Proof of university PI rights:** Being listed as a PI on a grant is not the same as having university PI status. You must have been conferred PI rights by the university. 2. **Proof of control of budget:** You must demonstrate that you control the budget for the research. If it is not clear that you have these rights, your request may be rejected. You can contact FASRC with further details if you have PI rights but your request is rejected for this reason. Source: [Request an FAS Research Computing Account](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account/)
✅ Expected Answer
A NON-FACULTY PI must supply a 33-digit billing code at signup. Non-Faculty PI accounts also carry additional controls and privileges, and the signup requires vetting so it takes longer to process.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

Question 24: question_24

❓ Question

Our lab has grant money and wants to buy extra compute nodes for the cluster. Can we just have them racked whenever we're ready?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/macaulay2, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.html, slurm.schedmd.com/SLUG23/NERSC-SLUG23.pdf

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, you cannot simply purchase and rack extra compute nodes on your own. FASRC manages all cluster hardware procurement, installation, and integration centrally. Here's why: - **Infrastructure management**: FASRC handles all aspects of cluster hardware, including procurement, rack installation, network integration (such as the NDR Infiniband interconnect), power, cooling, and ongoing maintenance [Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health – FAS Research Computing Overview](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview/). - **Standardization**: The cluster uses standardized node configurations and topologies. Mixing non-standard or lab-purchased hardware could create compatibility issues with the workload manager (Slurm), storage systems, and network fabric. - **Procurement process**: Any hardware additions go through FASRC's internal procurement and planning processes, which consider factors like: - Power and cooling capacity in the data center - Network fabric expansion - Software and licensing compatibility - Long-term maintenance and support contracts **What you can do instead:** 1. **Contact FASRC** to discuss your compute needs and explore options for additional resources. You can reach them through the [FASRC Portal](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu). 2. **Consider alternative options** such as: - Requesting additional allocation on existing cluster partitions - Using cloud resources (AWS, GCP, Azure) which FASRC may support - Exploring dedicated partitions if your lab has sustained high demand 3. **Optimize current usage**: Review [Job Efficiency and Optimization Best Practices](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices/) to ensure you're getting the most from your current allocation. If you have specific compute requirements, FASRC can help identify the best path forward for your lab's needs.

No. FASRC will discuss your computing needs, coordinate a quote, and install the new hardware, but as of the 2019 cluster refresh and the move to water-cooling, new nodes can be added into the cluster only at certain times of the year.

Question 25: question_25

❓ Question

If a Chan School PI sponsors an outside collaborator's cluster account, who ends up paying for that person's compute time, and what happens to their access if the PI leaves?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasrc-and-fasse-quick-start-guide-v2, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health (HSPH) and FAS Research Computing Q&A: **Who pays?** The outside collaborator's compute usage (CPU hours) is applied to the sponsoring PI's lab group fairshare and billed to the school (HSPH) on a quarterly basis. There is no up-front cost for creating the account, but the PI's lab group absorbs the compute costs. **What happens if the PI leaves?** If the sponsoring PI leaves HSPH, the outside collaborator's access would also end unless it is transferred to another PI's account and billing. It is the departing PI's responsibility to notify FASRC when the collaborator's access should be withdrawn. **Key requirements:** - The collaborator's account must be approved by and attached to an existing HSPH PI's FASRC account - When requesting the account, you must note that this is an outside collaborator (select 'EXTERNAL') [Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health + FAS Research Computing Q&A](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa/)
✅ Expected Answer
There is no up-front cost for the collaborator's account, but their cluster usage (in CPU hours) is applied to the sponsoring PI's lab group fairshare and billed to the school on a quarterly basis. The account must be requested as EXTERNAL and be approved by and attached to an existing HCSPH PI's FASRC account. Should the PI leave HCSPH, the collaborator's access also ends unless it is transferred to another PI's account and billing; it is the PI's responsibility to notify FASRC when the collaborator's access should be withdrawn.

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, I don't see specific information about whether Harvard Chan School (HSPH) users are charged a yearly per-person fee for Research Computing accounts. What I can confirm from the sources: - **Account access is sponsored**: You need a PI with an existing FASRC account to sponsor your request [Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health – FAS Research Computing Overview](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview). - **Billing is at the school/department level**: "Cluster usage and additional resources may be subject to charges to the PI, school, or department. All billing is done via Harvard internal billing codes at the Tub/school level" [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications). - **HSPH uses FASRC**: The Chan School is listed as using the FAS Research Computing environment [Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health – FAS Research Computing Overview](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview). However, the documentation doesn't specify whether there's a per-person annual fee for Chan School users specifically. For the most current and accurate information about HSPH-specific billing arrangements, I'd recommend: 1. Checking with your HSPH department administrator or PI about their current billing arrangement 2. Contacting FASRC directly at [https://www.rc.fas.harvard.edu/about/contact/](https://www.rc.fas.harvard.edu/about/contact/) 3. Reviewing the [HCSPH & FAS Research Computing Q&A](/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa/) page mentioned in the Chan School overview
✅ Expected Answer
No. The previous annual per-user charge for FAS Research Computing accounts is no longer used as of fiscal year 2019. Standard services — including accounts and access to the FASRC high-performance computing cluster — are effectively free to Harvard Chan School community members via the account of an eligible PI, because these FASRC computing costs are now covered by the school's overhead rate. Supplemental resources such as increased storage and VMs still involve additional charges.

Question 28: question_28

❓ Question

I'm on FASSE and want to run VS Code on a compute node. Can I just set up Remote-SSH to the compute node the way I do on Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/vscode_remote_tunnel.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/git-and-github, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/git-and-github-on-odyssey, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, you can't just copy the Cannon Remote-SSH setup to FASSE. The documented VS Code Remote-SSH configuration is specific to the **Cannon** cluster (it proxies through `cannon` login nodes and uses Cannon's SLURM partitions like `gpu_test` or `test`) [VSCode Remote Development via SSH and Tunnel](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel/). FASSE has a different cluster architecture and login node naming, so the `ProxyCommand` and host definitions won't work as-is. You would need to adapt the SSH config for FASSE's login nodes and SLURM partition names, or use the **Remote – Tunnel** approach instead, which FASRC recommends for resilience against network glitches [VSCode Remote Development via SSH and Tunnel](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel/). Would you like help adapting the setup for FASSE, or guidance on using the Remote Tunnel approach?
✅ Expected Answer
No. You cannot use Remote SSH to connect your local VS Code to a FASSE compute node, because salloc is not permitted on FASSE for security reasons. Remote SSH to FASSE's login node does work. For a FASSE compute node, launching a Remote Tunnel as a batch job (Approach I) is your only option — and before doing that you must enable internet access by setting a network proxy (you are on the FASSE VPN realm, @fasse), including adding the same proxy in your local VS Code settings; remember to unset that proxy in VS Code when you are not on the FASSE VPN, or it will block your other non-FASSE VS Code remote sessions.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Question 29: question_29

❓ Question

The VS Code Dev Containers extension keeps trying to talk to a Docker daemon on the cluster, which doesn't exist there. What do I have to change so it uses the container runtime FASRC actually provides?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/containers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, slurm.schedmd.com/containers.html, slurm.schedmd.com/containers.html, slurm.schedmd.com/containers.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The VS Code Dev Containers extension defaults to Docker, but FASRC clusters provide **Podman** instead. You need to reconfigure the extension to point at Podman's socket. ### Setup steps 1. **Install the extension** — [Visual Studio Code Dev Containers](https://marketplace.visualstudio.com/items?itemName=ms-vscode-remote.remote-containers) 2. **Change two settings** in *Code > Settings > Extensions > Dev Containers*: - **Dev > Containers: Docker Path** — change from `docker` to `podman` - **Dev > Containers: Docker Socket Path** — change from `/var/run/docker.sock` to `/tmp/podman-run-/podman/podman.sock`, replacing `` with your FASRC user ID (find it with `id -u` on the cluster) > ⚠️ **Note:** If you use a local VS Code (not via OOD Remote Desktop), you'll need to revert these changes when you want to use dev containers with a local Docker installation on your laptop. ### Recommended connection method The Dev Container extension is **not supported** in the web-browser-based VS Code (vscode.dev) with a remote tunnel. Use either: - **OOD Remote Desktop** to launch VS Code on the cluster, or - **Approach I: Remote – Tunnel via batch job** with a local VS Code client ### Known limitations - The `docker-in-docker` dev container feature is **unsupported** - The `docker-outside-of-docker` feature requires a workaround: start a Podman API service on the compute node with `nohup podman system service -t 0 >/dev/null 2>&1 &` and add socket-mount settings to your `devcontainer.json` For full details, see [VSCode Remote Development via SSH and Tunnel](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel).
✅ Expected Answer
Point the Dev Containers extension at Podman. In Code > Settings > Extensions > Dev Containers, change 'Dev > Containers: Docker Path' from 'docker' to 'podman', and change 'Dev > Containers: Docker Socket Path' from '/var/run/docker.sock' to '/tmp/podman-run-/podman/podman.sock', replacing with your FASRC user ID (obtained by running 'id -u' on the cluster). If you are using a local VS Code rather than an OOD Remote Desktop, you will need to revert these settings later if you use dev containers with a local Docker installation.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Question 30: question_30

❓ Question

I set up TF 2.16 on the cluster with the FASRC conda steps and now Keras blows up saying it has no backend. What environment variable am I supposed to export?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ood-remote-desktop-how-to-open-software, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/pytorch/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You need to export the `KERAS_BACKEND` environment variable: ```bash export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow" ``` This tells Keras to use TensorFlow as its backend. You can add this line to your shell profile (e.g., `~/.bashrc`) so it persists across sessions, or set it in your job scripts before running your code.
✅ Expected Answer
Export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow". Starting with version 2.16.1, TensorFlow includes Keras 3.0, so the Keras backend must be set to TF. FASRC's instructions include `export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow"` in both the GPU and CPU conda recipes, and prefix the singularity runs with it as well, e.g. `KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow" singularity exec --nv tf2.16.1_gpu.simg python3`.

Question 32: question_32

❓ Question

My PyTorch job on gpu_test sees a device but the card looks sliced up and I only get a fraction of it. What do I need to do differently on that partition?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/mmpose.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/pytorch/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
On the `gpu_test` partition, the GPUs are configured in **MIG (Multi-Instance GPU) mode**, which slices the physical GPU into smaller instances. This is why you see a device but only get a fraction of the card's resources. To use the GPU correctly on `gpu_test`, you need to explicitly set the `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` environment variable to point to the specific MIG instance allocated to your job. Here's what you need to do: 1. **Check which MIG instance you have:** ```bash nvidia-smi -L ``` 2. **Set `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` to the MIG instance:** ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-5b3... # replace with the actual MIG UUID from nvidia-smi -L ``` This step is **specific to the `gpu_test` partition** and is not necessary on the `gpu` partition, where GPUs are used in their full, unsliced form. For more details, see the [mmpose setup notes](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/mmpose.md) and the [PyTorch guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch).
✅ Expected Answer
gpu_test is currently the only FASRC partition with MIG (multi-instance GPU) mode enabled, so you must target a MIG instance explicitly. Get the GPU/MIG instance name with `nvidia-smi -L`, then set CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES to the MIG instance, e.g. `export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-5b36b802-0ab0-5f37-af2d-ac23f40ef62d`, or automate it with `export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$(nvidia-smi -L | awk '/MIG/ {gsub(/[()]/,"");print $NF}')`.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch
💬 Agent Messages (2)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: My PyTorch job on gpu_test sees a device but the card looks sliced up and I only get a fraction of it. What do I need to do differently on that partition?
🤖 Assistant Message #2
On the `gpu_test` partition, the GPUs are configured in **MIG (Multi-Instance GPU) mode**, which slices the physical GPU into smaller instances. This is why you see a device but only get a fraction of the card's resources. To use the GPU correctly on `gpu_test`, you need to explicitly set the `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` environment variable to point to the specific MIG instance allocated to your job. Here's what you need to do: 1. **Check which MIG instance you have:** ```bash nvidia-smi -L ``` 2. **Set `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` to the MIG instance:** ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-5b3... # replace with the actual MIG UUID from nvidia-smi -L ``` This step is **specific to the `gpu_test` partition** and is not necessary on the `gpu` partition, where GPUs are used in their full, unsliced form. For more details, see the [mmpose setup notes](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/mmpose.md) and the [PyTorch guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch).

Question 33: question_33

❓ Question

Our CryoSPARC master keeps coming up bound to the wrong machine name so the workers can't reach it. Where is that hostname actually set?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/bashrc_additions.sh, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmdbd.conf.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The hostname is set in the CryoSPARC master configuration file: **`cryosparc_master/config.sh`** Look for the `CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME` variable. For example: ```bash export CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME=holy_______.rc.fas.harvard.edu ``` You'll find this file at `$INSTALL_DIR/cryosparc_master/config.sh` (where `$INSTALL_DIR` is your CryoSPARC installation directory). After changing the hostname, restart the master so workers can reach it: ```shell cryosparcm restart ``` If you also changed the port number, you'll need to fix the database port mapping first: ```shell cryosparcm fixdbport cryosparcm restart ``` See the [FASRC CryoSPARC documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc/) and the [CryoSPARC config.sh reference](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md) for more details.
✅ Expected Answer
It is set in the CryoSPARC master config file, `cryosparc_master/config.sh`, which holds the license, the path to the MongoDB database, the master hostname, and the base TCP port. The hostname of the node running the master application is typically hardcoded in that config.sh; if it is left unset, CryoSPARC takes the hostname of the machine on which `cryosparcm start` is called. If these config.sh settings are not correct you will experience errors. Note also that the TCP ports of the individual component programs are not individually configurable — only the base port the user connects to is.

Question 34: question_34

❓ Question

Which processes does `cryosparcm start` bring up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/bashrc_additions.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/configure.sh, slurm.schedmd.com/burst_buffer.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The retrieved FASRC documentation doesn't list the specific processes that `cryosparcm start` launches. However, according to the official CryoSPARC documentation, `cryosparcm start` brings up the following core processes: 1. **CryoSPARC Master** — the central web application and API server 2. **MongoDB database** — stores CryoSPARC project and job data 3. **CryoSPARC Worker** — handles job execution (if configured on the same node) For a complete list of processes and their roles, see the official CryoSPARC management documentation [here](https://guide.cryosparc.com/setup-configuration-and-management/management-and-monitoring/cryosparcm). On FASRC clusters, the master is typically started on a CPU compute node, and separate GPU worker nodes are configured for job execution [CryoSPARC – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc/).
✅ Expected Answer
Running `cryosparcm start` brings up six applications: app (cli), command_core, command_rtp, command_vis, database (MongoDB), and webapp. At the top level cryosparcm is a Supervisor-based shell script that manages them, and they mostly communicate with one another over TCP.

Question 35: question_35

❓ Question

Google finally approved my AlphaFold 3 request and sent me the weights as a .bin.zst download. Where am I supposed to unpack that on Cannon — can I just leave it in netscratch?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/alphafold_v3.def, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/alphapose.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/run_af3_data_pipeline.sh, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, here's what you need to know about storing and unpacking your AlphaFold 3 model weights on Cannon: ### Where to store the model parameters The example scripts on FASRC's [User_Codes GitHub](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md) show model parameters being stored in a lab-specific directory, such as: ``` /n/holylabs/LABS//Lab/alphafold3/model_parameters ``` You should **not** leave the `.bin.zst` file in `netscratch` for long-term use. `netscratch` is intended for temporary, active work — model weights are large, persistent files that should live in a more permanent location. ### Recommended approach 1. **Transfer the `.bin.zst` file** to a more permanent directory (e.g., your lab's Holylabs directory or your home directory if space allows). 2. **Unpack it** using `zstd`: ```bash zstd -d your_model_weights.bin.zst ``` This will produce the `.bin` file(s) that AlphaFold 3 expects. 3. **Point your SLURM scripts** to the unpacked location via the `my_model_parms_dir` variable in the example scripts. ### Key references - The FASRC AlphaFold docs explain that model parameters must be requested from Google and stored in a directory you specify [AlphaFold – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold/) - The example SLURM scripts on [FASRC User_Codes GitHub](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md) show how to set `my_model_parms_dir` and bind it into the Singularity container ### Next steps - Confirm with your PI or lab admin where your lab's Holylabs directory is located - Check available space in that directory before transferring the weights - Let me know if you need help adapting the example SLURM scripts for your setup
✅ Expected Answer
Extract the file Google sends you with `unzstd file.bin.zst`, then place the resulting `file.bin` in a lab share — do NOT put it in netscratch. That location is what you pass as `--model_dir` when you run AlphaFold3.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold

Question 36: question_36

❓ Question

Before I can run an AlphaFold3 prediction on the cluster, do I need to download the AlphaFold databases into my own lab storage first? If they're already there, what's the path?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/introduction-to-cluster-online

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**No, you do not need to download the AlphaFold databases yourself.** FASRC has already placed the databases in a cluster-wide location: - **AlphaFold 3 databases:** `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v3` - **AlphaFold 2 databases:** `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v2` These are stored on SSD storage as recommended by the developers, so you can reference them directly in your job scripts without any local download step. **One additional requirement for AlphaFold 3:** You must obtain the model parameters from Google (see [Obtaining model parameters](https://github.com/google-deepmind/alphafold3/tree/main?tab=readme-ov-file#obtaining-model-parameters)). After downloading the `file_name.bin.zst` file from Google, extract it with `unzstd file.bin.zst` and place the resulting `file.bin` in a **lab share** (not netscratch). This will be the path you pass to `--model_dir` in your AlphaFold 3 run. Example scripts for running AlphaFold 3 (data pipeline on CPU, inference on GPU) are available in the [FASRC User_Codes repo](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/tree/master/Applications/AlphaFold).
✅ Expected Answer
No download is needed. The AlphaFold database is kept in a cluster-wide location (on SSD, as the developers recommend), so individual users do not have to download it. The AlphaFold3 database is at `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v3` (AlphaFold2's is at `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v2`). Likewise, the Singularity images are cluster-wide at `/n/singularity_images/FAS/alphafold/`, so you do not have to copy those either.

Question 37: question_37

❓ Question

I copied a friend's sbatch script and stripped it down — it has no partition line, no memory line, and no time line. What does the scheduler actually hand me?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/R/Example1/run.sbatch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/multicore.slurm, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/run.sbatch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/configure.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/basicbatch.slurm, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmdbd.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cli_filter_plugins.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_submit_plugins.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Cpp/Example6/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/Job_Arrays/job_arrays_1/test.py, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/README.md

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you omit `#SBATCH` directives for partition, memory, and time, SLURM applies the **default values** defined in your account’s SLURM configuration. Here’s what you typically get: - **Partition**: Falls back to the **default partition** for your account (often `standard` or `workq`, depending on cluster and your group’s settings). You can check your default partition with `sacctmgr show user format account,default_partition`. - **Memory**: Defaults to the **per-node or per-core memory limit** set in the SLURM config (commonly 2–4 GB per core on many FASRC clusters). If your job needs more, it will be killed with an `Out Of Memory` error. - **Time**: Defaults to the **maximum walltime** allowed for the default partition (often 24–48 hours). If you need less, you should still specify it to avoid holding resources longer than necessary. ### How to check your actual defaults Run these commands to see what your account is assigned: ```bash # Show your account and default partition sacctmgr show user format account,default_partition # Check resource limits for your account sacctmgr show account format MaxTRES,MaxJobs,MaxNodes # See current SLURM defaults for your user scontrol show config | grep -i "def" ``` ### Recommendation It’s best practice to **explicitly specify** partition, memory, and time in your script so you know exactly what you’re getting and avoid surprises. For example: ```bash #SBATCH --partition=standard #SBATCH --mem=8G #SBATCH --time=02:00:00 ``` This makes your job’s resource needs clear and reproducible. If you’d like, I can help you look up the specific defaults for your account or cluster.
✅ Expected Answer
FASRC's defaults when nothing is requested are: 1 core, 100 MB of memory, 0 GPUs, and the `serial_requeue` partition. Time is the exception — there is no default time limit, so you must always declare a time yourself. The 100 MB memory default is very likely to get your job killed, so you should always specify `--mem` or `--mem-per-cpu`.

Question 38: question_38

❓ Question

I have about 12,000 near-identical runs to do. Can I submit them as one --array job, and does that eat into the number of jobs I'm allowed to have queued?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, slurm.schedmd.com/openapi_release_notes.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes, you can submit 12,000 near-identical runs as a single job array, but there are important limits and considerations: ### Job Array Limits - Slurm has a configuration parameter called **MaxArraySize** that controls the maximum number of tasks in a job array. The default is typically 1,001, but the system maximum can be up to 4,000,001 [Slurm Job Array Support](https://slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html). - You'll need to check what **MaxArraySize** is configured to on FASRC clusters. If it's set to the default of 1,001, you'll need to request an increase or split your work into multiple arrays. ### Does it count against your queued job limit? - A job array is counted as **one job** in Slurm's job accounting, not 12,000 separate jobs. So it won't eat into your per-user queued job limit in the same way submitting 12,000 individual jobs would. - However, the array tasks still consume resources (CPUs, memory, nodes) when they run, and those resources count against your allocation. ### Practical considerations for 12,000 tasks - Use `--array=0-11999` (or your preferred indexing scheme) to submit the array. - Consider using a limit on simultaneous tasks with the `%` syntax, e.g., `--array=0-11999%100`, to prevent all 12,000 tasks from trying to run at once and overwhelming the scheduler. - Make sure your batch script uses the `$SLURM_ARRAY_TASK_ID` environment variable to differentiate between runs (e.g., for input/output file naming). Would you like help constructing the `sbatch` command with the array syntax?
✅ Expected Answer
No — you must split it. The maximum array size is 10,000 (that cap applies to both the array index and the array size), so a single 12,000-element array is not allowed. And yes, it counts against your job cap: each array index counts as a single job for the purposes of the Maximum Number of Jobs per User, which is 10,100.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 39: question_39

❓ Question

My analysis takes about 3 hours every time on the shared partition, but when I run the exact same thing on serial_requeue it keeps getting killed for hitting the wall clock. What time should I be asking for there?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Cpp/Example6/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/Job_Arrays/job_arrays_1/test.py

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the documentation, the `serial_requeue` partition is designed for short, serial jobs — typically those that run **5 to 10 minutes** or less. The guidance is to keep jobs on this partition brief so the scheduler can efficiently process them. Since your analysis takes **3 hours**, it is **not well-suited** for `serial_requeue`. That partition is meant for quick, individual tasks (like file compression, small data transformations, etc.), not long-running analyses. ### What to do instead: 1. **Stick with the `shared` partition** for your 3-hour jobs — that's what it's for. 2. If you need to break your analysis into smaller pieces that can run on `serial_requeue`, each piece should be **under ~10 minutes**. 3. If your 3-hour job keeps getting killed on `shared`, check whether you're requesting enough time. As a best practice, run a test job, check the actual runtime with `sacct` or `seff-account`, then round **up to the nearest hour** to account for variability ([Job Efficiency and Optimization Best Practices](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices/)). ### If you must use `serial_requeue`: You'd need to restructure your analysis into many smaller, independent tasks, each taking well under an hour (ideally 5–10 minutes). For a 3-hour job, that means breaking it into dozens of smaller pieces and submitting them as a job array or loop. Would you like help breaking your analysis into smaller tasks, or troubleshooting why it's being killed on `shared`?
✅ Expected Answer
Ask for roughly 9 hours. `serial_requeue` (and `gpu_requeue`) are mosaic partitions made of a wide variety of hardware, so run times vary a lot depending on which host you land on. The rule of thumb is a factor of three variance in speed — so a job that takes 3 hours on most hardware should be given 9 hours on `serial_requeue`. Alternatively, use the `--constraint` option to pin the job to a specific hardware type so the runtime is predictable.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices

Question 40: question_40

❓ Question

My PI bought the nodes in our lab's own partition, and we only ever run there. So why does our fairshare keep dropping — aren't we just using hardware we already paid for?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/introduction-to-cluster-online, slurm.schedmd.com/classic_fair_share.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html, slurm.schedmd.com/classic_fair_share.html, slurm.schedmd.com/fair_tree.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
This is a common point of confusion! The short answer is: **Fairshare is calculated community-wide, not per-partition.** Here's what's happening: 1. **Your "Share" is higher** — Because your PI bought nodes, your lab's **Share** (the portion of the system you're granted) is boosted above the base Gratis Share that every lab receives. This is documented in the [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/) guide. 2. **Fairshare score = Share vs. Usage** — The Fairshare score is calculated based on the ratio of your **Share** to your actual **Usage** across the entire cluster. If you're using a lot of resources (even on your own partition), your usage goes up, which can drive your Fairshare score down. 3. **It's a community-wide system** — The Fairshare system is designed to ensure all labs get their fair portion of the cluster and to prevent any single lab from monopolizing resources. As the docs state, "This keeps individual labs from monopolizing the resources, thus making it unfair to labs who have not used their fairshare for quite some time" [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). **The key insight:** Buying nodes increases your Share (your entitlement), but if you're actively using those nodes heavily, your Usage increases proportionally. The Fairshare score reflects whether you're using resources commensurate with your Share. If you're using more than your fair portion relative to other labs, your score drops. If you want to understand your specific Fairshare score, you can check it via the cluster dashboard or contact FASRC for consultation on budgeting your usage [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/).
✅ Expected Answer
Because Fairshare is a global quantity on Cannon, not a per-partition one: RawShares are granted to everyone and can be used anywhere on the cluster, so a user is charged regardless of which partition they run in. Groups that own private partitions are granted RawShares equivalent to the hardware in that partition, and that grant exactly offsets the usage of the partition — so it nets out. The upside of this design is flexibility: because the Share is global, a group can leave its own partition idle and spend its shares elsewhere on the cluster instead.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 41: question_41

❓ Question

Last week a bunch of our jobs died from node failures and we cancelled several more that were misconfigured. Does that wasted compute still count against our lab's score, and how long until it stops hurting us?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/job_state_codes.html, slurm.schedmd.com/resource_limits.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-defense-shield, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/introduction-to-cluster-online, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-defense-shield

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, I can provide some general guidance, but specific details about FASRC's scoring policy and decay timelines are not fully covered in the indexed sources. **Node failures:** According to the [Running Jobs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/) documentation, jobs that die from `NODE_FAILURE` are automatically requeued by the scheduler. This suggests that compute consumed by node failures may be handled differently than user-initiated cancellations. **Score decay:** The [Slurm Resource Limits](https://slurm.schedmd.com/resource_limits.html) documentation mentions that resource usage (GrpTRESMins) is "decayed (at a rate of PriorityDecayHalfLife)" and can also be "reset (according to PriorityUsageResetPeriod)." However, the exact values for these parameters on the FASRC cluster are not specified in the available documentation. **What you should do:** 1. Check your lab's current score and usage in the FASRC Portal or Coldfront 2. Contact FASRC Support directly ([support@rc.fas.harvard.edu](mailto:support@rc.fas.harvard.edu)) to ask specifically about: - Whether `NODE_FAILURE` jobs count against your score - Whether cancelled jobs count against your score - The exact decay timeline for your cluster's configuration The FASRC team can provide the most accurate answer regarding your specific lab's scoring policy and the current `PriorityDecayHalfLife` and `PriorityUsageResetPeriod` values in use.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, it still counts. RawUsage is the sum of all usage including failed jobs, jobs that are requeued, and jobs that ran on nodes that failed — FASRC counts it because it is the user's responsibility to use the allocated time effectively even when it is cut short. That usage does decay, though: RawUsage is attenuated by the cluster's half-life, currently 3 days. Work done in the last 3 days counts at full cost, work done 6 days ago costs half, work done 9 days ago one fourth, and so on. To limit the damage from failures, FASRC recommends testing/verifying code before running and enabling checkpointing so jobs can restart where they left off.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 42: question_42

❓ Question

I'm a master's student and my advisor wants me to run analyses on the Nielsen consumer panel data. Can I be added to the agreement?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/other-institutions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-use-agreements, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/roles-responsibilities

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the documentation, access to the Nielsen consumer panel data is managed through a specific process involving the Harvard Office of Sponsored Programs and Chicago Booth. Here's what you need to know: **The agreement is project-based, not individual-based.** The Harvard Office of Sponsored Programs manages registration codes and provides a Data Use Agreement (DUA) for **Researchers** (typically Principal Investigators). Before you can access the data, your advisor (as the PI) must: 1. **Register the project** on the Chicago Booth Nielsen site 2. **Submit data safety and DUA applications** in the Research Administration system 3. **Determine who handles certificate of destruction** after the data expires (your department IT or FASRC if using FASRC servers) 4. **If using FASRC resources**, submit a FASSE project request form with the DUA and DAT IDs **As a master's student**, you would typically work under your advisor's project. You don't get "added to the agreement" directly—rather, your advisor establishes the project and DUA, and then you can be granted access to use the data on FASRC resources through that project. **Next steps:** - Ask your advisor to initiate the DUA process through the Harvard Office of Sponsored Programs - Once the DUA is approved, you'll need to submit a FASSE project request if using FASRC servers - Data transfer happens via Globus For more details, see the [Nielsen Dataset documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset/).
✅ Expected Answer
No. Under the Master Access Agreement, the Nielsen data and license are made solely to the individual researcher registered via the Chicago Booth Nielsen site. That researcher can be a Ph.D. student or a postdoc who has a tenure-track faculty member registered on the site — but the data is explicitly not available to undergraduates or master's students. Relatedly, under the Data Access and Confidentiality Agreement the Researcher may disclose the Data only to PhD-seeking students of Harvard working under their supervision on the Research Project.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
