Question 44: question_44

❓ Question

I want to spin up a few hundred cores on Cannon to crawl a public website faster. It's for academic research — any issue with that?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-figure-out-how-efficient-my-job-is, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-security-levels, slurm.schedmd.com/rest.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — there are a few important issues to consider before using hundreds of Cannon cores for web crawling: ### 1. Web Scraping Policy FASRC has a specific [Web Scraping Policy](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy/) that you should review. Key points: - **Fair use does not generally cover programmatic machine scraping.** The policy distinguishes between human information gathering and automated scraping. - **You are using the target site's servers and bandwidth** in ways they may not approve, which can lead to IP blacklisting or even legal action. - **Academic purpose does not exempt you** from the usage policies of social media or other Internet platforms. - If you're scraping for the purpose of training a Generative AI (GAI) model, you must contact the Harvard Office of the General Counsel or the Office of the Vice Provost for Research. - If the data is sensitive, confidential, or contains human data, it needs compliance review before being placed on the FASRC cluster. ### 2. Cluster Resource Limits Submitting a job for "a few hundred cores" on Cannon will need to go through Slurm and comply with FASRC's [Acceptable Use Policy](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use/). Large multi-core jobs are subject to: - Partition-specific limits (core/node caps, walltime limits) - Fairshare scheduling priorities - Potential impact on other users' jobs ### Recommendations 1. **Review the Web Scraping Policy** linked above and consider whether your use case is appropriate for cluster resources. 2. **Contact FASRC support** or your department's research computing liaison to discuss your job size and whether Cannon is the right platform for this workload. 3. **Consider whether a smaller-scale approach** (fewer cores, longer walltime, or using a different resource) would be more appropriate. Would you like help figuring out the right Slurm job parameters or partition to target?
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, several. FASRC highly discourages using the cluster itself to scrape: because of its size and ease of parallelization, the cluster is easily weaponized and your actions could have consequences for other researchers, so you should seek another avenue for data acquisition first. You must contact FASRC before commencing any scraping activity using the FASRC cluster. It is highly preferable that you do the scraping elsewhere and then bring the data to the cluster for processing — and if the data is sensitive, confidential, contains human data, or it is unclear, doing it elsewhere is a requirement. Highly parallelized scraping is specifically called out as risking blacklisting of the cluster's or the university's IP range, which would harm other users. Being an academic does not exempt you from a site's usage policies.

Question 45: question_45

❓ Question

I logged in to Globus and picked the Harvard FAS RC Holyoke collection, but I can't browse into my home directory and the Everyone folder in my lab share doesn't show up either. Is my account misconfigured?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center-bill

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
This is expected behavior — your account is not misconfigured. **Home directories are not available in Globus.** Per the [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/) documentation, home directories are intentionally excluded from Globus under all circumstances because "sharing home directories creates a security vulnerability, as authentication keys/passwords are stored there." **Regarding the "Everyone" folder in your lab share:** If you don't see it, it likely means you don't have explicit access to that share. Lab shares in Globus are only visible to users who have been granted access. You'll need to be added as a user or have the share explicitly shared with you. Check with your PI or lab manager to confirm your access permissions on the lab storage.
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is misconfigured — both are expected. Home directories are not and will not be added to Globus under any circumstances, because sharing home directories creates a security vulnerability (authentication keys/passwords could be shared inadvertently). And within a lab share, Globus can only see the `Lab` and `Users/$USER` folders — anything outside those, such as `Everyone` and `Transfer`, is not available through Globus (note the `Users` folder has been deprecated as of 2025). Also be aware symlinks do not work in Globus. If your lab share genuinely lacks a `Lab` directory or is not visible at all in the Holyoke/Boston collections, contact FASRC.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Question 46: question_46

❓ Question

I need to move a directory holding roughly half a million small output files off the cluster with Globus. What's the recommended way to package that up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center-bill, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
For a directory with ~500,000 small files, FASRC strongly recommends **packaging the data into a tar archive** before transferring via Globus. Transferring hundreds of thousands of individual files is slow and prone to interruption, whereas a single tar file transfers more quickly and is much more resilient to transfer failures ([Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/)). ### Recommended approach 1. **Create a tar archive** of the directory on the cluster. Use the guidance in [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/) to plan your archive strategy. 2. **Keep archives reasonably sized** — avoid creating one massive archive. If your data is large, split it into multiple tar files so that a single corruption doesn't destroy your entire dataset. 3. **Generate a checksum file** alongside the tar archive to verify data integrity after transfer. 4. **Transfer the tar file(s)** via Globus instead of the individual files. ### Why tar? - **Faster transfers**: Globus handles a single file much more efficiently than hundreds of thousands of small files. - **Resilience**: If the transfer is interrupted, you can resume from a single file rather than thousands of individual transfers. - **Tape compatibility**: If you're archiving to tape, tarred files are smaller and transfer more efficiently when retrieved ([Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/)). Would you like help with the specific `tar` command or Globus setup for your data?
✅ Expected Answer
Tar the directory into subset files first. FASRC recommends that your tar files range in size from 1-100 GiB — that size range transfers more quickly (especially if the transfer is interrupted) and is smaller to retrieve if it ever needs to come back from Tape. If you leave hundreds or thousands of files in a single directory, Globus will go into an 'endpoint is too busy' state and your job will timeout, restart, timeout, etc. FASRC also recommends reading their 'Tips for using tar to archive data' page before tarring.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Question 47: question_47

❓ Question

I ran shasum on a folder before I tarred it up, and when I extracted the archive later and hashed it again the values don't match, even though every file looks fine. What am I doing wrong?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmdbd.conf.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The most common reason for mismatched checksums after `tar` extraction is that **the paths used for the checksum are different**. For example, if you hash a folder using its full path: ```bash find /home/username/myscans/ -type f -print0 | xargs --null -o shasum | sort | shasum ``` But then extract the archive into a relative directory and hash it with: ```bash find myscans/ -type f -print0 | xargs --null -o shasum | sort | shasum ``` The resulting checksums will differ, even though the file contents are identical. This is because the full paths are embedded in the `shasum` output, and `sort` orders them differently. **Recommendation:** Always use the exact same path format when computing checksums before and after extraction — ideally just the directory and sub-directory in question, rather than mixing absolute and relative paths. If you can't do this consistently, checksumming may not be reliable for your use case. Also note: you cannot store the checksum file *inside* the tar archive itself, because its presence would affect the checksum. You'd need to remove it after extraction and before re-running the checksum. See [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/) for more details.
✅ Expected Answer
The checksum depends on the path you hand to find, so the same files hashed two different ways produce two different values. FASRC's guidance is to always do it the exact same way: cd to the directory *above* the one you are archiving (e.g. /n/mypath/scans) and then run find against the relative directory name (myscans) — not the full path. Two other things will break the comparison: (1) modifying the data after you took the checksum but before you tarred it (a checksum is useless on an active filesystem), and (2) storing the checksum file itself inside the tar, since its presence changes the checksum unless you remove it after un-tarring and before re-running.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving
💬 Agent Messages (2)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid

🤖 Assistant Message #2

Question 48: question_48

❓ Question

RC made a tar of my project with Starfish. I copied it to netscratch and extracted it there, but instead of my ProjectX folder I got this weird nested tree of empty directories with ProjectX way down at the bottom. Is the archive corrupt?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/7zip, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
This is a common issue with tar archives and it's **not necessarily corrupt**. The problem is almost certainly that the RC team created the tar archive using **absolute paths** (e.g., `/n/holyscratch01/username/ProjectX/...`) instead of relative paths. When you extract a tar archive that contains absolute paths, tar will recreate the full directory structure starting from the root, which is why you're seeing empty directories all the way down to your actual `ProjectX` folder. **To fix this, you have a few options:** 1. **Extract to the correct location** - If the archive contains absolute paths like `/n/holyscratch01/username/ProjectX`, you can extract it directly to the root: ```bash tar -xvf archive.tar -C / ``` This will recreate the full path structure. 2. **Use `--strip-components`** - If you want to remove some levels of the directory structure, you can use: ```bash tar -xvf archive.tar --strip-components=N ``` where N is the number of directory levels to strip from the beginning of each path. 3. **Check the archive contents first** - Before extracting, see what's in the archive: ```bash tar -tvf archive.tar | head -20 ``` This will show you the full path structure and help you determine the right extraction approach. **To prevent this in the future**, when creating tar archives, always use relative paths or specify the directory you want to archive rather than using absolute paths. For example: ```bash cd /n/holyscratch01/username/ tar -cvf archive.tar ProjectX/ ``` Instead of: ```bash tar -cvf archive.tar /n/holyscratch01/username/ProjectX/ ``` The FASRC documentation on [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/) recommends being careful about how you specify paths when creating archives to avoid these issues.
✅ Expected Answer
The archive is fine. Starfish tars in place and captures the entire path inside the tar file, so extracting recreates that whole path underneath your output directory. Extracting a Starfish tar of /n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX into /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp gives you /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX. Move the ProjectX directory to where you actually want it, then remove the leftover empty n/holylabs/jharvard_lab tree. Use plain `rm` rather than `rm -rf`, so it errors out if other files remain or you typed the wrong path.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving
💬 Agent Messages (6)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: RC made a tar of my project with Starfish. I copied it to netscratch and extracted it there, but instead of my ProjectX folder I got this weird nested tree of empty directories with ProjectX way down at the bottom. Is the archive corrupt?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🛠️ Tool Call #3: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: tar archive nested directories empty directories when extracting
🛠️ Tool Call #4: search_local_files
Args: RC tar archive nested directories empty directories
🛠️ Tool Call #5: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: RC tar archive created with absolute paths nested directories
🤖 Assistant Message #6
This is a common issue with tar archives and it's **not necessarily corrupt**. The problem is almost certainly that the RC team created the tar archive using **absolute paths** (e.g., `/n/holyscratch01/username/ProjectX/...`) instead of relative paths. When you extract a tar archive that contains absolute paths, tar will recreate the full directory structure starting from the root, which is why you're seeing empty directories all the way down to your actual `ProjectX` folder. **To fix this, you have a few options:** 1. **Extract to the correct location** - If the archive contains absolute paths like `/n/holyscratch01/username/ProjectX`, you can extract it directly to the root: ```bash tar -xvf archive.tar -C / ``` This will recreate the full path structure. 2. **Use `--strip-components`** - If you want to remove some levels of the directory structure, you can use: ```bash tar -xvf archive.tar --strip-components=N ``` where N is the number of directory levels to strip from the beginning of each path. 3. **Check the archive contents first** - Before extracting, see what's in the archive: ```bash tar -tvf archive.tar | head -20 ``` This will show you the full path structure and help you determine the right extraction approach. **To prevent this in the future**, when creating tar archives, always use relative paths or specify the directory you want to archive rather than using absolute paths. For example: ```bash cd /n/holyscratch01/username/ tar -cvf archive.tar ProjectX/ ``` Instead of: ```bash tar -cvf archive.tar /n/holyscratch01/username/ProjectX/ ``` The FASRC documentation on [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/) recommends being careful about how you specify paths when creating archives to avoid these issues.

Question 49: question_49

❓ Question

I already pushed a bunch of archives into our lab's tape area on Globus and then realized I named them badly. Can I just rename them, or move a couple into a different folder to tidy things up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center-bill

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Unfortunately, the documentation doesn't explicitly address renaming or moving files **after** they've been staged/migrated to tape. Here's what I can tell you based on how tape systems typically work: **Key points:** 1. **Tape staging area vs. tape itself** — You're likely writing to a disk-based staging/buffer area first, and then the data is migrated to tape. The staging area is what you interact with via Globus [Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/). 2. **Before migration to tape** — If your files are still in the staging area (not yet migrated to tape), you should be able to rename or move them using Globus File Manager just like any other file transfer. 3. **After migration to tape** — Once data has been migrated to tape, renaming or moving files is generally not supported in the same way as on disk. Tape systems typically don't support arbitrary file operations post-migration. **What to do:** - Check in Globus whether your files are still in the staging area or have already migrated to tape - If they're still in staging, you can rename/move them via Globus File Manager - If they've already migrated to tape, you may need to contact the Storage Service Center for guidance on whether retrieval and re-transfer is possible Would you like help checking the status of your transfers or reaching out to the Storage Service Center?
✅ Expected Answer
No — do not delete, move, or rename data once it has been transferred to the tape buffer. The Globus buffer gives you write access to upload data destined for tape, and all changes within your lab's Globus collection are automatically picked up by the tape robot. Deletions on the Globus buffer may result in permanent deletion on tape, and deleting files that have already been placed on tape removes the 'link' and makes retrieval impossible. Mass deletions, moves, and file renaming require cumbersome manual intervention. Name files appropriately before you transfer them, and work with your PI or lab data manager on your lab's tape storage protocols.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Question 50: question_50

❓ Question

Our PI wants to let a postdoc read and write only one project folder inside our lab's tape collection on Globus, not the rest of it. How do we set that up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center-bill, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/sequencing-information, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rclone, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on FASRC documentation, here is how the PI can grant the postdoc read/write access to only one project folder within the lab's tape collection: ### The Solution: Create a Globus Guest Collection Globus allows you to share a specific **subfolder** (rather than the entire tape collection) by creating a **Guest Collection**. This is the standard way to restrict access to a single project folder. **Steps for the PI (or General Manager):** 1. **Get the Postdoc's Globus Account:** Obtain the email address associated with the postdoc's Globus identity. 2. **Go to Globus File Manager:** Log in to the [Globus File Manager](https://app.globus.org/file-manager). 3. **Select the Folder:** Navigate to the specific project folder inside the tape collection that the postdoc should access. 4. **Create Guest Collection:** * Select the folder and click the **"Share"** icon (or right-click and select "Share"). * Click on **"Add Guest Collection"**. * Fill in the information for the new Guest Collection and click **"Create Collection"** [Globus File Transfer – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer). 5. **Grant Permissions:** * In the **"Permissions"** tab of the new Guest Collection, click **"Add Permissions — Share With"**. * Enter the postdoc's Globus account email to share the collection with them [Globus File Transfer – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer). 6. **Postdoc Access:** The postdoc will receive a notification email and will then be able to initiate file transfers to/from that specific shared folder [Globus File Transfer – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer). ### Important Notes on Tape Access * **Default Permissions:** By default, only the PI and General Manager have access to the tape collection via Globus [Globus: Transfer Data to Tape – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access). * **Lab Members:** The PI or General Manager can add other lab members to access the tape, but the entire group does not have access by default [Globus: Transfer Data to Tape – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access). * **Staging Area:** Users write to a disk-based staging/buffer area via Globus, and the data is then migrated to tape [Globus: Transfer Data to Tape – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access). For detailed step-by-step instructions on creating Guest Collections, FASRC recommends the official Globus tutorial: [How To Share Data Using Globus](https://docs.globus.org/guides/tutorials/manage-files/share-files/).
✅ Expected Answer
You cannot. There is no way to restrict permissions on individual folders within your lab tape collection — read/write access applies to the whole lab collection on Globus. The underlying filesystem is not owned by FASRC, so FASRC cannot set those permissions; everyone with access is essentially a guest on the NESE tape system. (The PI and General Manager can add lab members to the collection, but only to all of it.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Question 51: question_51

❓ Question

On Cannon I just do salloc to grab a shell on a compute node, but on FASSE it doesn't work. How am I supposed to get an interactive session there?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/developing-apps-on-ood, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
On FASSE, you should use **`srun`** instead of `salloc` to get an interactive shell. For example: ```bash srun -p test -t 10 --mem 1G --pty /bin/bash ``` This allocates resources and starts an interactive shell on a compute node. The `--pty` flag allocates a pseudo-terminal, giving you an interactive session. As noted in the FASRC documentation, `salloc` should not be used on FASSE for interactive sessions [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/). Would you like help with specific resource requests (partition, time, memory, or GPUs)?
✅ Expected Answer
salloc is deliberately unavailable on FASSE — it is disabled for security reasons, and FASSE does not allow interactive jobs from the command line at all. For interactive work you use the FASSE Open OnDemand (OOD) / VDI virtual desktop at https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu, reachable in your browser while connected to the FASRC VPN on the @fasse realm (note @fasse, not the @fasrc realm you use for Cannon). OOD gives you pre-built apps and interactive command-line access in a desktop-like environment. SSH to fasselogin.rc.fas.harvard.edu remains available for batch work.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

❓ Question

How long can a job run on the FASSE H200 GPU nodes, and how many GPUs does each of those nodes have?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/seas-compute-resources, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasrc-ai-facilitation, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/kempner-partitions, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/run.sbatch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/seas-compute-resources, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation: - **Time limit:** Jobs on the `gpu_h200` partition (FASSE H200 nodes) have a maximum runtime of **3 days** [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/). - **GPUs per node:** Each node has **4 NVIDIA H200 GPUs** [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/). When submitting jobs, you'll need to request GPUs using `#SBATCH --gres=gpu:n` where `n` can be 1–4 per node [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/).
✅ Expected Answer
The fasse_gpu_h200 partition has a 3-day time limit — shorter than the 7 days allowed on the other FASSE partitions. There are 2 such nodes, each with 4 H200 GPUs, 112 Intel 'Sapphire Rapids' cores, 990 GB of memory per node, and 843 GB of local /scratch.

Question 53: question_53

❓ Question

A data provider will only let me download their dataset if I give them an IP address to add to their allowlist. What address do they see when I pull the data from a FASSE compute node?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-use-agreements, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you download data from a FASSE compute node, the external server sees the IP address of the FASRC proxy that your traffic exits through, not the compute node's internal IP. You can find the exact public IP address by running this command on a compute node: ```bash curl icanhazip.com ``` This will return the IP address that the internet sees you coming from, which is the address you should provide to the data provider for their allowlist [FASSE Proxy Settings](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings/). Note that FASSE uses a proxy for internet access, so all outbound traffic from compute nodes goes through this proxy, and the data provider will see the proxy's IP address.
✅ Expected Answer
140.247.232.228. All traffic to the Internet from FASSE — VPN, login, compute, or fasseood — goes out through the FASRC proxy (rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu), which currently has that external IP address. It is subject to change, so you can confirm it by running `curl icanhazip.com` from the node you will actually be working on.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings

Question 54: question_54

❓ Question

Our data use agreement says the data has to be encrypted at rest. I was planning to stage the intermediate files for my FASSE jobs in netscratch since it's fast. Is that OK?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center-bill, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**No, you cannot use netscratch (or any scratch space) for data covered by a DUA that requires encryption at rest.** According to the FASSE documentation on protected data transfers: > "If you have a DUA that requires encryption at rest, you must not use scratch for any data that the DUA applies to. Neither local scratch, nor our global scratch, support encryption at rest." [FASSE / Protected Data Transfers](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers/) This applies to both local scratch on the compute nodes and global scratch (netscratch). You'll need to use a storage location that supports encryption at rest for your intermediate files. Would you like help identifying which storage options on FASSE support encryption at rest?
✅ Expected Answer
No. If you have a DUA that requires encryption at rest, you must not use scratch for any data the DUA applies to — neither local scratch nor FASRC's global scratch supports encryption at rest. Separately, DSL 3 / FASSE data should not be stored in your home directory either. You are responsible for knowing and complying with the applicable Harvard Information Security Policy, the Harvard Research Data Security Policy, and any applicable contracts / data use agreements; if you are unsure how this applies to your data, consult FASRC at security@rc.fas.harvard.edu or your School Security Officer.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers

**No, you cannot use netscratch (or any scratch space) for data covered by a DUA that requires encryption at rest.** According to the FASSE documentation on protected data transfers: > "If you have a DUA that requires encryption at rest, you must not use scratch for any data that the DUA applies to. Question 55: question_55

❓ Question

Our level 3 project storage doesn't show up as a collection in Globus at all. What do we have to do to get it added?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, **data security level 3 storage shares are not automatically added to Globus** ([Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/)). To get your level 3 project storage added, you need to **contact FASRC** directly. The documentation states: > "If you find that your lab share is not seen by Globus or these sub directories don't exist in your lab's share, in the Holyoke or Boston collections, please contact us." Specifically, level 3 data should appear under either: - **"Harvard FAS RC Holyoke Secure"** (for Holyoke data center) - **"Harvard FAS RC Boston Secure"** (for Boston data center) Reach out to FASRC support to request the addition of your level 3 storage to Globus.
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is broken: DSL 3 / FASSE storage is intentionally not included in Globus by default. To have it exposed, first review the governing DUA — Globus makes data available over the Internet without a VPN or private network, so a DUA requiring VPN/private-network access for all access to the data would not be Globus-compliant (consult your School Security Officer if unsure). Then submit a ticket to rchelp@rc.fas.harvard.edu that (1) includes the path to the project to add (e.g. /n/piname_project_l3), (2) states that the PI attests Globus is compliant with any contracts/DUAs governing the data, and (3) is either sent by the PI or receives a direct reply from the PI confirming it.

❓ Question

How much CPU and RAM am I actually allowed on the FASRC login server, and is there a cap on how many terminal windows I can have open at once?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/trinity, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ood-remote-desktop-how-to-open-software, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/Large_Data_Processing_R/parallel_computation/R_parLapply_vdi.md, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cgroup.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/Large_Data_Processing_R/parallel_computation/R_embarrassingly_parallel.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vpn-setup, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster, slurm.schedmd.com/pam_slurm_adopt.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/jupyter-notebook-server-on-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rclone, slurm.schedmd.com/MISC25/Slinky-CUG2025.pdf, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-to-a-compute-node, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/access-and-login, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/virtual-desktop, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/AITools/ollama.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/access-from-abroad, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmd.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sackd.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/glossary, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/developing-apps-on-ood

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on FASRC documentation, here are the limits for the login nodes: ### CPU and RAM Limits The Rocky 8 login nodes use a system-level mechanism called **cgroups** which limits each logged-in account to: * **1 CPU core** * **8 GB of memory** [Don't run anything on the login nodes](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities/) If a process exceeds these limits, it will be killed without warning. Login nodes are intended only for light processes like preparing jobs, editing files, or monitoring jobs—not for actual computation. If you need more resources, you should request an interactive job session on a compute node. ### Terminal Windows The documentation does not specify a hard cap on the number of terminal windows (SSH sessions) you can have open at once. However, because each session consumes resources (up to the 1 core / 8 GB limit per account), opening many sessions that run background processes could cause you to hit those resource limits and get killed. For light use (just keeping shells open or running very small tasks), you likely won't hit a session count limit, but you should monitor your resource usage.
✅ Expected Answer
Each login-node session is limited to 1 core and 8GB of memory, and you may have at most 5 sessions per user. Users who abuse the login nodes may have their sessions terminated, and the login nodes are rebooted during monthly maintenance to clear out stale sessions. If you need more than 5 sessions, switch to batch jobs, use Open OnDemand (which gives you a desktop with multiple terminals on a dedicated compute node), or use screen/tmux to multiplex one session.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

Question 57: question_57

❓ Question

I ran ssh-copy-id and my key works great when I'm in the office, but from my apartment it still makes me punch in the 6-digit code every single time. Did the key not install properly?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-to-a-compute-node, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasrc-and-fasse-quick-start-guide-v3, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasrc-and-fasse-quick-start-guide-v2, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/introduction-to-cluster-online, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/sftp-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vpn-setup, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the documentation, **your SSH key likely installed correctly** — the 6-digit code you're seeing is your **two-factor authentication (OpenAuth/Duo) token**, not a passphrase on your SSH key. Here's what's happening: - When you're **on the office network**, FASRC likely recognizes your connection as coming from a trusted/internal network, so it may skip or bypass the two-factor challenge for SSH connections. - When you're **off-campus (from your apartment)**, FASRC requires the full authentication flow: SSH key → password → **two-factor code** (the 6-digit code from your OpenAuth/Duo Mobile app). This is normal security behavior — your SSH key authenticates *who you are*, but the two-factor code is an additional layer that's required when connecting from untrusted networks. **What you can do:** 1. **Use the VPN** — If you connect via the FASRC VPN first, your connection will appear to come from the trusted office network, and you may be able to bypass the two-factor prompt. See the [VPN setup documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vpn-setup/) for details. 2. **Keep using Duo Mobile** — As documented in [Using Duo Mobile to display your RC two-factor code](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile/), you can use Duo Mobile to generate the 6-digit OpenAuth token for off-campus SSH connections. 3. **Verify your key is installed** — You can confirm your key is working by checking that you're only prompted for the 6-digit code (not a password) after the key authentication succeeds. The key itself is working fine — you're just seeing the expected two-factor authentication requirement for off-campus connections.
✅ Expected Answer
The key is fine — this is by design. Passwordless (ssh-key) authentication is only permitted to the Cannon login nodes from Harvard networks, including Harvard/FASRC VPNs. If you connect from an external network you are still required to provide two-factor authentication regardless of your key. So either connect to the VPN from home, or accept the 2FA prompt. (On FASSE, passwordless ssh is permitted from the FASSE VPN only.) You can confirm the key installed by checking that it is present in ~/.ssh/authorized_keys on the cluster.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the documentation, here is the breakdown for your Level 2 survey data: ### 1. Which cluster? You should be on the **Cannon** cluster. * **Cannon** is rated for **Level 2** data (and below) [IQSS Cannon Quickstart Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide/). * **FASSE** is the secure environment reserved for **Level 3** data. You should **not** use FASSE for Level 2 data, and you should not transfer Level 3 data from FASSE into Level 2 space on Cannon [FASSE Cluster (FAS Secure Environment)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse/). ### 2. What goes in the boxes? When connecting to Cannon, you perform two distinct steps: **Step A: The VPN Box** * **Server Address:** `vpn.rc.fas.harvard.edu` * **Username:** Your FASRC username followed by `@fasrc` (e.g., `jharvard@fasrc`) * **Password:** Your FASRC password * **2FA Token:** Your FASRC 2FA token (generated via your FASRC 2FA setup, **not** your HarvardKey code) [IQSS Cannon Quickstart Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide/). **Step B: The Cluster Login Box** * **URL:** `https://rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu` (Open On Demand) * **Username:** Your FASRC username **only** (do **not** include `@fasrc`) * **Password:** Your FASRC password [IQSS Cannon Quickstart Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide/).
✅ Expected Answer
Level 2 data belongs on Cannon, the FAS research computing cluster for Data Security Level 2 (only Level 3 data must move to the FASSE secure environment). To get on: in the Cisco AnyConnect VPN client use server address vpn.rc.fas.harvard.edu and enter your FASRC username in the format username@fasrc, your FASRC password, and your FASRC 2FA token — note this is NOT the code associated with your HarvardKey account. Then log into the Cannon cluster at https://rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu using just your FASRC username WITHOUT the '@fasrc', plus your FASRC password.

Question 59: question_59

❓ Question

My pandas script keeps blowing up with a memory error because the file is bigger than the node's RAM. Does FASRC want me to rewrite it, or can I keep using pandas somehow?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/Example2/run.sbatch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise1/Python/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/PnetCDF/Example3/block_cyclic.f90, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/trinity, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
FASRC doesn't require you to abandon pandas, but you do need to process the data in chunks rather than loading the entire file into memory at once. Here are the main approaches: ### 1. Use `chunksize` with `read_csv` ```python import pandas as pd chunk_iter = pd.read_csv('large_file.csv', chunksize=100_000) results = [] for chunk in chunk_iter: # Process each chunk processed = chunk.some_operation() results.append(processed) final_result = pd.concat(results) ``` This keeps only one chunk in memory at a time. ### 2. Use Dask as a drop-in replacement [Dask](https://docs.dask.org/) provides a pandas-like API that works out-of-core (on disk) and can scale to multiple cores: ```python import dask.dataframe as dd ddf = dd.read_csv('large_file.csv') result = ddf.groupby('column').mean().compute() ``` ### 3. Request more memory If your file is only slightly larger than available RAM, you can request a larger memory allocation in your SLURM job script: ```bash #SBATCH --mem=64G # request more RAM per node ``` Check available node sizes and partitions on the [FASRC documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm/) to pick an appropriate allocation. ### 4. Use more efficient file formats Consider converting to **Parquet** or **HDF5**, which support columnar storage and can be read more efficiently: ```python # Write once df.to_parquet('large_file.parquet') # Read with filtering df = pd.read_parquet('large_file.parquet', columns=['col1', 'col2']) ``` ### Recommendation - For **small to moderate** oversize: increase `--mem` in your job script. - For **large files**: use `chunksize` or switch to Dask. - For **repeated analysis**: convert to Parquet/HDF5 for faster, more memory-efficient reads. Let me know what size your file is and what operation you're doing, and I can suggest a more specific approach.
✅ Expected Answer
Both options exist, and which one FASRC recommends depends on scale. Start with chunking. For moderately large datasets, Dask can enhance Pandas by parallelizing operations — you can convert a Pandas DataFrame to a Dask DataFrame with dd.from_pandas() and keep most of your existing code. But for very large datasets that exceed memory constraints, using Dask alone as a substitute for Pandas is the more effective solution: Dask is a drop-in replacement with a familiar DataFrame API that works lazily, breaking work into smaller tasks that only execute when you call .compute(). Separately, switching from CSV to a columnar format like Parquet (e.g. df.to_parquet('output_data.parquet', compression='snappy')) greatly improves I/O performance.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Question 60: question_60

❓ Question

I switched to the newest Intel module on the cluster and now the compiler command I've always used doesn't exist anymore. What am I supposed to call to build my C file now?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cpp-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/MPI/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/WRF_Model/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cpp-programming-language

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The compiler command changed when Intel moved to the **oneAPI** toolchain (Intel 24.0.0 and newer). Here's what to use: | Intel module version | C compiler command | |---|---| | **Below 24.0.0** (e.g., `intel/23.0.0-fasrc01`) | `icc` | | **24.0.0 and above** (e.g., `intel/24.0.1-fasrc01`) | **`icx`** | So if you loaded the newest Intel module (24.x), use **`icx`** instead of the old `icc`: ```bash module load intel/24.0.1-fasrc01 # or whatever the latest version is icx -O2 -o myprogram myprogram.c ``` You can check which Intel versions are available with: ```bash module spider intel ``` And then load the specific version you want. See the [C Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language/) page for more details.
✅ Expected Answer
Intel renamed the compiler binaries at version 24.0.0. For Intel modules below 24.0.0 the commands are icc (C), ifortran (Fortran), and icpc (C++). For Intel 24.0.0 and above they are icx (C), ifx (Fortran), and icpx (C++). So with a newer Intel module you must invoke icx instead of icc. (You can list the available Intel compilers with `module spider intel` and load a specific one, e.g. `module load intel/23.0.0-fasrc01`.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Question 61: question_61

❓ Question

Is it fine to build my C code right after I ssh in, and should I just put the gcc line at the top of my job script so it's always fresh?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cpp-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-to-a-compute-node, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/centos7-singularity

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No to both ideas: 1. **Don't build right after SSHing in** — compilation can take seconds to a minute, so you'll tie up the login node. The best practice is to jump to a compute node (via `salloc` or `srun`) to compile, or compile separately before submitting a batch job [C Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language/). 2. **Don't put the `gcc` line at the top of your job script** — this recompiles the program every time you submit the job. Instead, compile once separately, then use the resulting executable in your `sbatch` script. If you change the source code, recompile separately and then submit the production run as a batch job [C Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language/). In short: compile once on a compute node (or interactively), then submit the executable via `sbatch`.
✅ Expected Answer
No on both counts. FASRC recommends jumping to a compute node to compile a C program (compilation can take a few seconds to a minute depending on the code's complexity), and specifically recommends using the `test` partition to compile and test the program before running the production job. Best practice is to compile the code separately and then use the resulting executable in your sbatch script — avoid putting the compilation command inside the sbatch script, because that recompiles the program on every submission. If you change the source, recompile separately and then resubmit the production batch job.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Question 62: question_62

❓ Question

I want to completely wipe my Julia install off the cluster and start clean. Is there a single command, and does it leave junk behind in my home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/bash, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Julia/Notebook.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Julia/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, there is **no single "uninstall" command** for a Julia installation. Here's what you need to know: ### How Julia is installed on the cluster Julia is typically installed locally in your `$HOME` directory using the installer from [julialang.org/downloads](https://julialang.org/downloads/). The installer: - Places Julia binaries in `$HOME/.juliaup` (or similar) - Adds `juliaup` initialization lines to your `~/.bashrc` ### To completely remove Julia You'll need to do two things manually: 1. **Remove the Julia installation directory** (usually `~/.juliaup` and `~/.julia`): ```bash rm -rf ~/.juliaup ~/.julia ``` 2. **Clean up your `~/.bashrc`** — remove the lines that Juliaup added to your PATH. These typically look like: ```bash # juliaup eval "$(juliaup shellintegration bash --save-quiet)" ``` ### Does it leave junk behind? Yes, if you only delete the Julia binaries, it will leave: - `~/.juliaup/` — the Juliaup package manager and installed versions - `~/.julia/` — package registries, compiled artifacts, and package data - Modified `~/.bashrc` — PATH initialization lines So a truly clean wipe requires removing all three. For more details, see the [Julia Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia/) documentation.
✅ Expected Answer
Run `juliaup self uninstall`. That is the uninstall command, but it does not always clean everything up: afterward, check for the `~/.julia` and `~/.juliaup` directories and delete them manually if they are still there. (This matters because the default Julia install location is $HOME, and the installer also adds Juliaup initialization to your ~/.bashrc to put `julia` on your $PATH.)

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia

Question 63: question_63

❓ Question

I chmod 777'd a file in my lab's share so a collaborator in a different lab group could grab it, but they still get permission denied when they try to read it. What's going on?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/unix-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/can-i-share-my-account, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/additional-groups, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-ownership-and-access-policy

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the documentation, here's what's happening: ## The Problem: POSIX Permissions Are Hierarchical On FASRC storage, file access is governed by **POSIX-style permissions** with three layers: **Owner**, **Group**, and **Other** (see [Understanding file and group permissions](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions/)). When you `chmod 777` a **file**, you're granting read/write/execute to everyone — but **directory permissions also matter**. To access a file, a user must have **execute (x) permission on every parent directory** in the path to that file. If the parent directory (or any ancestor) has restrictive permissions (e.g., `drwxr-x---`), users outside the owning group **cannot traverse into it**, regardless of what the file's own permissions are. ## Why Your Collaborator Gets "Permission Denied" 1. **Directory permissions block access** — Even if the file itself is `777`, the directories leading to it may only allow access to your lab group. Your collaborator, being in a different lab group, can't pass through those directories. 2. **Lab shares use group-based access** — Lab directories and tiered shares are typically set up with group ownership (e.g., `drwxrws--` for the Lab directory) so that only members of your lab group can access the data. Users from other labs are in the "Other" category, which often has no permissions. ## How to Fix It You have a few options: 1. **Use POSIX ACLs** — Instead of `chmod`, use `setfacl` to grant specific users or groups access to files and directories: ```bash setfacl -m g:other_lab_group:rX /path/to/file ``` 2. **Add your collaborator to your lab group** — If appropriate, request that your PI add them as a temporary member of your lab group. 3. **Use Globus or another transfer mechanism** — [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/) is designed for sharing files between different groups. 4. **Create a shared directory with appropriate permissions** — Place the file in a location where both groups have access, or use a shared project directory. Would you like help with any of these approaches?
✅ Expected Answer
Making the file world-readable is not enough on its own. For a user in the 'Other' category to reach a file, they must first be able to traverse into the top-level share/directory that contains it. If the top level of the lab directory or share is set drwxr-x---, drwxrwx---, or drwxr-s--- then Other has no access to the share at all, even if a file inside it is set -rwxrwxrwx. Other must first be able to 'see' into the directory/share; otherwise it has no access. (FASSE/L3 shares should never allow Other access.) To share with someone outside the group, the enclosing directory must permit Other traversal - e.g. the 'Everyone' directory that lab shares provide for exactly this purpose - or the person can be added to your lab Group by your PI.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Question 64: question_64

❓ Question

My labmate keeps his personal scratch folder under Users/ in our old lab share, but our newly created share and netscratch have no Users folder. Where am I supposed to put my own personal directory now?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Per FASRC documentation, the `Users` directory **only exists on shares created prior to October 2024**. On newly created shares and `/n/netscratch`, you should use the **`Lab`** directory to create your personal folders instead [Understanding file and group permissions – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions/). So for your new share or netscratch, create your personal directory directly under the `Lab` folder (e.g., `/path/to/share/Lab/your_username/`).
✅ Expected Answer
The Users directory only exists on shares created prior to October 2024. On new shares and on /n/netscratch there is no Users directory - FASRC says to use the Lab directory to create personal folders instead. The Lab directory is set drwxrws--- so that every member of the lab group has read/write/execute access to share data amongst the group, and it is the preferred place both for personal folders on new shares and for sharing data with your lab group. On the older shares, the sub-directories in the Users directory are owned by individuals and by default only allow execute access to the group.

Question 65: question_65

❓ Question

I finished the cluster account request form and it never asked me to choose a password. How do I actually get a password to log in?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-signup

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/additional-groups, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasrc-and-fasse-quick-start-guide-v2, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/introduction-to-cluster-online, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/get-a-fasse-account-and-project-group, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasrc-and-fasse-quick-start-guide-v3, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasrc-and-fasse-quick-start-guide-v2, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vpn-setup, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/get-a-fasse-account-and-project-group, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/virtual-desktop

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer

Stale session data or cached authentication tokens are a common cause of pages that accept credentials but never render. Try: - **Hard refresh**: `Ctrl+Shift+R` (Windows/Linux) or `Cmd+Shift+R` (Mac) - **Clear cache/cookies** for `portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu` specifically, then try again - Try an **incognito/private window** to bypass any cached data ### 2. If none of these steps resolve the issue, you should contact FASRC support with details about your browser, OS, and any error messages you see in the browser's developer console (F12 → Console tab).

Question 67: question_67

❓ Question

My R job asks Slurm for 8 cores, but when it runs my parallel code spawns dozens of workers and the node grinds to a halt. How am I supposed to determine the worker count inside the script?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cpu_management.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cpu_management.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scrun.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/EP/Example1/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise1/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hybrid-mpiopenmp-codes-on-odyssey, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/OpenMP/Example1/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The agent attempted to resolve the issue of R parallel workers exceeding allocated Slurm resources by searching for the correct environment variables (specifically `SLURM_NTASKS` and `SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK`) that define the number of available cores. The search confirmed that Slurm passes the task count via `SLURM_NTASKS` and CPU count via `SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK`. **Answer:** To determine the worker count inside your R script, you should read the `SLURM_NTASKS` environment variable, which represents the total number of tasks (cores) allocated to your job. In R, you can retrieve this value using: ```r n_cores <- as.integer(Sys.getenv("SLURM_NTASKS")) ``` If you specifically need the number of CPUs per task (which might differ if you requested multiple threads per task), use `SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK`. However, for most parallel backends like `parallel` or `future`, `SLURM_NTASKS` is the standard metric for the number of parallel workers to spawn. **Note:** This run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50.
✅ Expected Answer
Do not use parallel::detectCores() to determine the number of CPUs/processes to use in your job - it detects the number of physical CPU cores on the compute node, not the number allocated to your job, which is why you end up oversubscribed. Use parallelly::availableCores() instead, which returns the number of CPUs allocated to your Slurm job.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel

Question 68: question_68

❓ Question

I had openmpi loaded, then loaded mpich later in the same session, and now openmpi is gone from my module list. Did I corrupt my environment, and can I keep both around?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/MPI/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/MPI_2021/Example4/build.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/MPI_2021/Example2/build.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/MPI_2021/Example5/build.sh

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You haven't corrupted your environment — this is expected behavior. On the FASRC cluster, **OpenMPI and MPICH are mutually exclusive** because they provide the same set of MPI commands and libraries, and the module system is designed so that loading one will unload the other to avoid conflicts. From the FASRC documentation: > "There is no default MPI library in your environment when you log in to the cluster. You need to choose the desired MPI implementation for your applications. This is done by loading an appropriate MPI module. Currently the available MPI implementations on our cluster are OpenMPI and Mpich." [MPI (Message Passing Interface) & OpenMPI](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface/) ### Can you keep both around? Not simultaneously in the same shell session. The module system will automatically swap one for the other when you load a different MPI implementation. This is intentional to prevent linking against the wrong MPI libraries. ### What to do: - If you need to switch back to OpenMPI, simply load it again: ```bash module load openmpi/ ``` - If you need both implementations available, you can load whichever one you need at the time you compile or run your code. Just make sure you're using the correct MPI compiler wrappers (`mpicc`, `mpirun`, etc.) that correspond to the loaded module. For reference, here are some common module combinations: ```bash # GCC + OpenMPI module load gcc/13.2.0-fasrc01 openmpi/5.0.2-fasrc01 # GCC + MPICH module load gcc/13.2.0-fasrc01 mpich/4.2.0-fasrc01 ``` See [MPI Software on the FASRC cluster](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/MPI/README.md) for more examples.
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is corrupted - this is intentional. The modules are set up so that you can only have one MPI module loaded at a time; if you try loading a second one it will automatically unload the first, which is done to avoid dependency collisions. So you cannot keep both loaded: pick one implementation (OpenMPI or MPICH, each built with either the GNU or the Intel compiler suite) and load it with its matching compiler module, e.g. module load gcc/13.2.0-fasrc01 openmpi/5.0.2-fasrc01. Note also that there is no default MPI library in your environment when you log in - you must load one.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

This is done by loading an appropriate MPI module. Currently the available MPI implementations on our cluster are OpenMPI and Mpich." [MPI (Message Passing Interface) & OpenMPI](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface/) ### Can you keep both around? Not simultaneously in the same shell session. The module system will automatically swap one for the other when you load a different MPI implementation. This is intentional to prevent linking against the wrong MPI libraries. ### What to do: - If you need to switch back to OpenMPI, simply load it again: ```bash module load openmpi/<version> ``` - If you need both implementations available, you can load whichever one you need at the time you compile or run your code. Question 69: question_69

❓ Question

I need mpi4py in a conda environment, but it has to be built against the same OpenMPI my Fortran code uses. I just ran mamba create with mpi4py in the package list - what MPI did that actually give me, and how do I get OpenMPI instead?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface, slurm.schedmd.com/mpi_guide.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/Python/mpi4py/Example1/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-io, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/Python/mpi4py/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you run `mamba create` with `mpi4py` from the default (conda-forge) channels, it pulls in **`mpich`** as the MPI backend — **not OpenMPI** [MPI (Message Passing Interface) & OpenMPI – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface/). This is because the conda-forge `mpi4py` package depends on `mpich` by default. ### How to get OpenMPI instead You need to explicitly request OpenMPI when creating the environment so that `mpi4py` is built against it: ```bash module load python/3.10.12-fasrc01 mamba create -n python3_env1 python numpy pip wheel mpi4py openmpi source activate python3_env1 ``` By including `openmpi` in the package list, conda/mamba will resolve `mpi4py` against the OpenMPI libraries instead of MPICH. ### Verify which MPI you got Once activated, you can check: ```bash conda list | grep -E "mpi4py|mpich|openmpi" ``` This will show you exactly which MPI implementation your `mpi4py` is linked against. ### Important note If your Fortran code is using a **module-loaded** MPI (e.g., `module load mpi/openmpi4`), that MPI is separate from the conda environment's MPI. For true compatibility, you'd want both the Fortran code and the Python `mpi4py` environment to use the same MPI implementation. If your Fortran code is compiled against a system/module OpenMPI, consider building your conda environment with the matching OpenMPI version, or use the cluster's module-loaded MPI with `mpi4py` installed in a way that links against it (which may require a more manual build approach).
✅ Expected Answer
Putting mpi4py in the mamba create package list (e.g. mamba create -n python3_env1 python numpy pip wheel mpi4py) also installs an MPI library required by mpi4py, and by default that is MPICH - not OpenMPI. If your workflow requires a specific flavor and/or version of MPI, install mpi4py with pip into a custom conda environment instead: load the compiler and MPI modules first (module load gcc/12.2.0-fasrc01; module load openmpi/4.1.5-fasrc03), load a Python module (module load python/3.10.12-fasrc01), create the environment WITHOUT mpi4py (mamba create -n python3_env2 python numpy pip wheel), activate it (source activate python3_env2), then run pip install mpi4py so it builds against the loaded OpenMPI.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Question 70: question_70

❓ Question

I used the 'copy mount path to clipboard' button in Starfish and got /n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab, but when I cd to it on a login node I get 'No such file or directory'. What's the correct path?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding, slurm.schedmd.com/namespace.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/helmod-faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The path Starfish copies includes two pieces of metadata that need to be removed for use on the command line: 1. **`vast-`** — this prefix is only used in the Starfish UI. 2. **`/C`** (or `/F`) — this indicates which cluster the storage lives on (`C` = Cannon, `F` = FASSE). So from the Starfish path: ``` /n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab ``` Remove `vast-` and `/C` to get the correct command-line path: ``` /n/holylabs/jharvard_lab ``` You can then `cd` to it on a login node: ```bash cd /n/holylabs/jharvard_lab ``` This is documented in the [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/) guide.
✅ Expected Answer
The path Starfish copies is not directly usable - you must make two edits: remove 'vast-' and remove '/C'. So /n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab becomes /n/holylabs/jharvard_lab. The letter C indicates the filesystem is on the Cannon cluster; a group share on FASSE would show F instead of C before the group name.

Question 71: question_71

❓ Question

I want to drag a few files out of my home directory into my lab's netscratch folder using a point-and-click interface, but my home directory doesn't show up as an endpoint in Globus. What else can I use?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Unfortunately, your home directory is **not available as a Globus endpoint** under any circumstances. According to FASRC documentation, "Home directories are not and will not be added to Globus under any circumstances" because sharing them would create a security vulnerability (authentication keys, passwords, etc. could be exposed) [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). For moving files from your home directory to your lab's netscratch folder, here are your options: 1. **Use the command line** — You can use standard Unix commands like `cp`, `mv`, or `rsync` to transfer files between your home directory and lab storage on the cluster: ```bash cp /n/homeNN/yourlogin/somefile /n/scratchNN/labshare/somefile ``` 2. **Use Open On Demand (OOD)** — FASRC provides a graphical file manager through Open On Demand that lets you browse and move files between directories on the cluster via a point-and-click interface. You can access it at [https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/) and use the "Files" app [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/). 3. **Mount your home directory locally** — If you need a desktop point-and-click interface, you can mount your home directory on your local machine and then use Globus to transfer from your lab share endpoint. See the [mounting storage documentation](/kb/mounting-storage/) for details. For a few files, the command line or Open On Demand are the quickest solutions.
✅ Expected Answer
Globus does not have access to home directories, which is why yours does not appear. For transfers to or from your home directory, use an Open OnDemand Remote Desktop session instead: go to the OOD dashboard (Cannon: rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu, FASSE: fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu), start a Remote Desktop session, open a File Manager by clicking the 'Home' folder on the desktop (or Applications -> File Manager), open a second File Manager window, navigate one to the source (e.g. /n/home01/jharvard/) and the other to the destination (e.g. /n/netscratch/jharvard_lab/Lab/), then right-click the source file -> Copy and right-click the destination window -> Paste (drag-and-drop works too, but behaves like cut-and-paste). Command-line tools (cp, mv, rsync, fpsync) are the alternative; Globus is only recommended for transfers between different filesystems, not within one.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Question 72: question_72

❓ Question

My collaborator is at another university and doesn't have a HarvardKey. Can she still get an account on the cluster, and is there anything I have to keep doing so her access doesn't get shut off?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasrc-and-fasse-quick-start-guide-v2, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/offboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-signup

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the documentation, here's what you need to know about your collaborator's access: ## Yes, your collaborator can get an account As an external collaborator without a HarvardKey, she can apply for an **external FASRC account** through the [account request tool](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/request/account). You (as her sponsoring PI) can sponsor her account even though she doesn't have a Harvard appointment [Onboarding Policies and Procedures](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding/). ## You must re-enable her account every 90 days External accounts need to be **re-enabled every 90 days**. You will need to **request an extension every 90 days** to prevent her account from being suspended [Onboarding Policies and Procedures](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding/). ## Your responsibilities as sponsor - You are responsible for the usage and conduct of your external collaborator [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/) - The cluster should only be used for Harvard-based research, including work with external collaborators on jointly held projects [Responsibilities](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities/) - If she no longer needs the account, you should notify FASRC to close it, especially if it incurs charges or has access to sensitive data [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/) ## Alternative: Globus file sharing If she only needs to share files rather than access the cluster directly, you can use [Globus](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/) to transfer files with external collaborators without requiring them to have FASRC accounts. Would you like help with the account request process or setting up the 90-day extension reminders?
✅ Expected Answer
Yes. A collaborator without a HarvardKey can apply for an external FASRC account through the account request portal (https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/request/account). External accounts have to be re-enabled every 90 days: the PI must request an extension every 90 days or the account will be suspended. Faculty can sponsor FASRC accounts for anyone working in their lab, including external collaborators.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding
